‘Dig’ Review: Amy Poehler’s Talent-Rich Peacock Comedy Barely Scratches the Surface

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The great benefit of having clout in Hollywood — aside from fame, fortune, and a standing reservation at any restaurant in town — is being able to say “no.” You don’t have to take any job that pays. You don’t even have to work year-round. And you certainly don’t have to chase silly trends, like, say — I don’t know — making a comedy that’s also a mystery that’s also a drama? Beloved, funny folks like Amy Poehler and Mike Schur, who earned a huge chunk of their influence through the indelible sitcom, “Parks and Recreation,” should be able to make anything they want! Any way they want! They earned it!

Far be it from me to know what exactly went wrong with Schur and Poehler’s new series, but something did, unfortunately, go wrong. Each 32-35 minute episode strains to wrap up plots that limp to the finish line. Narrative momentum only exists from the minor twists revealed in the final seconds — then the credits roll, the “up next” button lights up, and your natural curiosity over what happens next wins out over your desire to scroll for something better. Worst of all, everything is “light”: The tone is light, the jokes are light, the mystery is light, the drama is light, even the lighting is light. (It’s so bright, all the time, even at night!) Watching “Dig” is like bearing witness to dwindling interest: Whatever originally compelled the “Dig” team to adapt Kate Myers’ 2023 novel, “Excavations” gets spread so thin across the disparate parts of this broad adaptation that there’s almost nothing left.

Nothing, that is, but the stars. Poehler leads an affable cast (astutely assembled by casting directors Allison Jones and Emily Buntyn) as lead archaeologist Elise, a devoted digger (no, not that kind) whose decades in the dirt have earned her respect and admiration but also turned her cynical and closed-off. She’s an accomplished expert in the field who’s been waiting to run her own site for years, working diligently under the tutelage of beloved British professor Neville Wycombe (Hugh Laurie), but events have conspired to keep her from the top job.

The most obvious issue is also the simplest: Neville has repeatedly promised to retire, only to rescind that promise when it comes time to make it official. This year he swears he’ll step down for real, but Elise knows better than to buy-in, especially when her informal audition (giving the welcome speech to a group of undergrads they call Ugs) exposes her greatest weakness: She’s not an inspiring leader. “I know your default setting is ‘tough love,'” Neville tells Elise, “but please learn to give people some grace.”

That advice is put to the test when Dylan (Geraldine Viswanathan), Elise’s flaky former protégé, shows up at the dig site after abandoning her post five years prior without so much as a goodbye. Returning with a questionable back tattoo that reads “a traveler on the adventure of life” and without any luggage, directions, or direction (as in, for her life), Dylan is a messy 29-year-old trying to sort things out as she turns 30. Luckily, she meets the too-put-together-for-her-own-good Clare (Antonia Thomas), a 39-year-old head preservationist who’s secure in her calling, engaged to Dylan’s ex-boyfriend Gary (Jon Pointing), and always dressed to the nines. (Surprise, surprise: Clare comes from money.)

The two will learn from each other, just as they’ll each learn from Elise, but they should all take more cues from Patty Borkman (Fina Strazza), the 19-year-old intern who’s assigned to be Neville’s assistant. That’s, in part, because Patty is the youngest and eagerist of a central foursome neatly divided by decades (did I mention Elise is 49?), but more so because Patty — as you may have guessed by her last name — is a fizzy vat of quirky energy that Strazza impressively dissipates into a charming, fresh, coming-of-age character.

Patty’s voice sounds like a shy version of Cristin Milioti’s adult baby from “30 Rock.” She wears silly hats, can’t drive, and struggles to stick up for herself. She kinda, sorta believes she’s a demigod (after reading one of Neville’s books on Greek mythology as a kid), and she’s always been an outcast (for these reasons and more). Watching Patty come into her own — in ways both delightfully weird and universally endearing — is the most rewarding and compelling aspect of “Dig”; a strange, distinctive spark that could grow into a roaring fire if the series continues (and learns from its strongest asset).

But that’s a big “if.” The first season (consisting of 10 episodes) pivots around the four women finding a rare statue and making the questionable decision to keep it secret from Neville. At the time, they say it’s because their boss takes credit for everything, and they want something that’s just their own, but that contention doesn’t hold up to scrutiny as the season progresses because Neville doesn’t hold up to scrutiny as the season progresses. Neville is whoever he needs to be in any given episode — a mentor, an oppressor, a goofball, a stick in the mud, a nice old man, and a villainous monster — and while Laurie can pivot with the best of them, his inconsistent character development betrays a show that’s hesitant to commit to much of anything.

The love triangle between Gary, Dylan, and Clare fizzles out without any substantial conflict. Elsewhere, an inappropriate crush is introduced and tossed away without consequence. Great guest stars like Jason Mantzoukas (a local looter) and Max Greenfield (as a sketchy art dealer) feel like they were hired for arcs that ended up getting cut, while regulars (like Gary) can feel adrift in their ample, listless screentime. Premises primed for comic hijinks never get there, mysteries teed up for suspense are left slack, and relationships that should create a little friction when brought together instead find a way to slide off each other without anything sticking.

Poehler, at the center of it all, is a problem. While it’s theoretically interesting to see the former Leslie Knope take the opposite tact — playing another hard-working public servant (every artifact they uncover remains in Greece) only without the unbridled optimism — Elise’s glum demeanor limits her ability to crack jokes, stir passion, and generally light up a scene. Patty is the only outsized character in a bunch of overly composed normies, and while everyone gets a moment or two to strut their silly stuff, it’s not enough in a series where the humor is already spread so thin.

“Dig” has all the talent it needs to be whatever show it wants to be. The most obvious option would be a comedy, but I have faith in the cast and creatives to excel in other genres, too. They just need to pick one and, when they do, dig a whole lot deeper than this.

“Dig” premieres Monday, November 23 on Peacock. All 10 episodes will be released at once.

Why Lenore Dove Baird From The Hunger Games: Sunrise On The Reaping Looks So Familiar

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

In 2025, "Hunger Games" author Suzanne Collins released one of the most long-awaited stories in the entire franchise — the backstory of District 12 victor Haymitch Abernathy, "Sunrise on the Reaping." A film adaptation titled "The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping" immediately took shape, and after Joseph Zada was cast as a young version of Haymitch — before he's an aging, depressed, drink-swilling lout portrayed by Woody Harrelson in the four original films — the rest of the cast began to round out.

Immediately after I read the book, I wondered who might be cast as Lenore Dove Baird, the beautiful and wild girlfriend Haymitch is forced to leave behind in District 12 when he's essentially chosen to participate in the Games by mistake. I was then extremely pleased when Whitney Peak was cast in this important part, who's not only Haymitch's love interest but a vital link to the other prequel "The Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes" ... in that Lenore Dove is distantly related to that film and novel's heroine Lucy Gray Baird (Rachel Zegler).

So where have you seen Peak before she took on the role of Lenore Dove? I should say that, in general, Peak's star is still on the rise, but there's a reason she earned this role; not only is she extremely talented, but she's appeared in some pretty high-profile projects in recent years. From a major HBO reboot (that did fail, by no fault of Peak's) to some independent films, here's where you've seen Whitney Peak, who plays Lenore Dove in "The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping," before.

After small roles in films like "Molly's Game" and TV shows like "The Chilling Adventures of Sabrina," Whitney Peak booked her first lead role courtesy of the "Gossip Girl" reboot that aired on HBO Max from 2021 to 2023.

As a huge fan of the ridiculous campfest that is the original "Gossip Girl" — which was adapted, very loosely, from a novel series written by Cecily von Ziegesar and ran on The CW from 2007 to 2012 — I was excited for this reboot, but sadly, it was a largely toothless and lackluster impersonation of the original. Still, Peak was very good! She plays Zoya Lott, a newcomer to the high-status New York City preparatory school who finds out that her late birth mother had another daughter. After Zoya and her half-sister, the wealthy and popular Julien Calloway (Jordan Alexander), make plans to meet up, they end up attending Constance Billard together ... but Zoya, who doesn't come from extreme privilege, struggles to fit in with Julien and her high society friends.

Again, this version of "Gossip Girl" is entirely too concerned with social justice crusades, whereas in the original show, the 20-somethings did insane stuff like sell each other for hotels. (Social justice is ... just not the point of a show like "Gossip Girl!") It was ultimately canceled after its Season 2 finale aired, leaving the story unfinished ... but it also freed Peak up for bigger and better opportunities.

In terms of her big-screen work, Whitney Peak has appeared in a solid mix of independent project and a few bigger movies — aside from "The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping." In 2022, Peak showed up in the supporting role of Becca in the legacy sequel "Hocus Pocus 2," and I already mentioned that she was in "Molly's Game," which marked her film debut.

Elsewhere, Peak led films like the independent horror movie "Eye for an Eye," directed by Colin Tilley and adapted from Elisa Victoria's book "Mr. Sandman" — in that, Peak plays a young woman grieving the tragic loss of her parents who finds herself newly traumatized by a shocking, violent incident in her new home in Florida. Peak also appeared in the action thriller "Trap House" alongside Sophia Lillis, Inde Navarrette, Jack Champion, and Bobby Cannavale, as well as Tommy Wirkola's survival film "Thrash" with Phoebe Dynevor and Djimon Hounsou.

You can see Peak as Lenore Dove Baird in "The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping" in theaters now.

GTA 6 Doesn't Have Any Sex Workers, and Rockstar Has Explained Why

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

A mainstay of the GTA series has been the presence of sex workers, but Rockstar Games has dropped this aspect for the newly released GTA 6.

As reported by Shack News, unlike most other games in the long-running franchise, you cannot hire a sex worker in GTA 6.

The outlet asked Rockstar about this decision, and received the following response:

"GTAVI is a game about a couple in a bind, lovers on the run. While we have taken a lot forward from previous GTA titles, everything we have implemented in VI has been done to support the spirit of the story we wanted to tell, and the gameplay systems we wanted to expand upon - things like wanted system, the radio, robberies and car theft. Many aspects of previous titles are not present in GTAVI, but the elements we have we believe are all necessary and complementary to the feeling and motivations we wanted to emphasize as you play through Jason and Lucia's story."

So, in other words, sex workers aren't included this time around because it wouldn't make sense for Jason or Lucia to pursue that, as they're already in a relationship — and that's a big part of the story.

As mentioned, they have been present in the series since 2001's GTA 3, and this will be the first 3D game to exclude them.

GTA 6 is also the first game that centres on a couple, however, with the relationship between Jason and Lucia interwoven in the gameplay as well as being a key tenet of the narrative. It seems Rockstar didn't think sex workers would be conducive to this goal.

A Full Timeline of Ella Langley and Tucker Wetmore’s Rumored Romance, Because It’s Very Confusing

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

If you’re a country music fan (or tbh, even if you’re not), then you’re probably well aware that the love lives of country music’s rising stars are quite entangled…and have been for a minute now. The latest (reported) update? Ella Langley and Tucker Wetmore are rumored to be dating, but we really can’t talk about their maybe-relationship without discussing Tucker’s ex Bryana Ferringer...or Ella’s rumored ex Riley Green (who went on to maybe date Bryana).

Ella and Tucker may have sparked dating rumors late last year, but it wasn’t without some drama on the way. It’s a pretty confusing ride, so buckle up and let’s get into it. Starting with…

After months of dating rumors following the success of Ella and Riley’s song “You Look Like You Love Me,” Ella finally shuts down Riley dating rumors. “We’re not dating,” she tells Taste of Country, adding, “We’re just good friends.”

Riley also shuts down dating speculation during an appearance on The Viall Files podcast. “Ella’s probably too smart to date me anyway,” he jokes, later adding, “When we first started having the success we did with that song, I kinda told Ella, I was like, ‘There’s gonna be a lot of things said, but I mean, you don’t really have to play into it or not play into it.’”

Tucker posts a TikTok on August 20 with the caption “@ my ex,” sparking breakup rumors with his model girlfriend Bryana (who, btw, appeared in his music video for “Wind Up Missin’ You”).

Soon after, Bryana is spotted with Riley at his Red Rocks tour stop.

Ella and Tucker both attend the 2025 BMI Country Awards on November 18, though it’s unclear whether this is the first time they ever meet (or if they even interacted, despite standing right next to each other in this group photo):

A day later, after several reported sightings around Nashville, Riley brings Bryana as his date to the CMA Awards on November 19.

However! Riley later refers to Bryana as merely a “friend,” telling Backstage Country, “It’s gonna be tough to tiptoe out of having a date to an award show. Yeah, I mean, you go to award shows all the time, and I’m always by myself, or I’ve got, like, a security guy, or a buddy with me, so I took a friend with me to the award show.”

Around the same time, Ella sparks dating rumors with Farmer Wants a Wife season 3 star John Sansone (whose cousin, music exec Sophia Sansone, previously dated Riley). Rumors begin after John includes a picture of Ella’s dog, Crue, in a November 26 Instagram photo dump...and then, on the same day, Ella includes a video of herself shooting shotguns with John in a photo dump of her own.

John later confirms to Taste of Country in April 2026 that he and Ella “dated for a couple of months” after he slid into her DMs but are no longer together.

Oh, and side note: John’s ex from Farmer Wants a Wife, Samantha Riehl, posted a TikTok with Tucker back in April 2025 with the caption, “Blondes are the worst.”

And then right after, John posted a TikTok with Bryana at Stagecoach (which has since been deleted).

Pretty sure nothing ever happened between Samantha and Tucker (or between Bryana and John), but still, it feels worth mentioning!!

On December 5, Tucker releases “Proving Me Right,” which fans are preeeetty convinced is about his ex, Bryana.

A few weeks later, Ella and Tucker are photographed together in the British Virgin Islands at the Soggy Dollar Bar...but considering the photo also includes other country artists, rumors circulate that the trip was possibly a writing retreat.

Tucker and Ella reunite to sing a live acoustic duet of Tucker’s “Sunburn” at the Heart Strings for Hope charity event on March 10.

And while Tucker didn’t write “Sunburn” himself, a few of the lyrics kiiiinda seem to allude to his December getaway with Ella.

Tucker then appears on the March 17 episode of Kristin Cavallari’s Let’s Be Honest podcast, where he claims that he has “never been broken up with” and has always been the one to “call it quits.” When Kristin asks if he has “sworn off blondes,” Tucker says, “Yep. I have. For the foreseeable future. That’s right.”

And then, a few days after that on March 21, Tucker posts an Instagram photo of himself holding a fish, and Ella slides into his comments with, “Cute fish.” Flirty!

Tucker discusses his hit “Brunette” with Audacy, saying, “My mom’s always said, ‘You're going to mess around with blondes for a little while but I truly believe you're going to settle down with the brunette,’ and I was like, ‘Yeah, 100 percent.’”

Not long after, during an appearance on Taste of Country Nights, Tucker notes, “Pretty much every girlfriend I have ever had was blonde, and I think it’s time to change up a couple of things.”

On September 23 (just a day after Ella breaks Mariah Carey’s record for longest-running Billboard Hot 100 hit with “Choosin’ Texas”), TMZ breaks the news that Tucker and Ella are (reportedly) dating.

The outlet obtains photos of the two out with friends in Nashville on the night of September 22 following the Nashville Songwriter Awards, with sources telling TMZ that the two met “through the music industry and mutual friends.”

Multiple sources also confirm the rumored romance to People so…it seems legit to me!

While making an appearance at WAVE in Wichita, Kansas, a fan straight-up asks Tucker whether he and Ella “are a thing.”

Tucker’s two-word response? “Next question.” Okay!!

Ella and Tucker seemingly take another trip to the Virgin Islands…at least, according to a sneaky fan who spotted them entering a restaurant together.

Soon after, they attend the CMAs, where they walk the carpet separately but still can’t dodge questions about their relationship status. When asked by People if there is a certain brunette he’s rooting for, he jokingly answers, “Zach Top or Stephen Wilson Jr.” Mmmkay!

Ella is also caught grinning during Tucker’s performance, which...isn’t confirmation of anything but certainly telling.

52-Episode 'War Of The Worlds Meets The Walking Dead' Delivers Sci-Fi At Its Finest

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Although a blend of The Walking Dead and War Of The Worlds sounds tough to pull off, one five-season hit managed to execute this very combination with aplomb. If The Walking Dead’s 177-episode run proves anything, it is that the show’s blend of post-apocalyptic devastation, intimate character drama, and gruesome zombie horror clearly connected with viewers.

This is without even mentioning Z Nation, Netflix’s All of Us Are Dead, The Last of Us, Kingdom, and the many other zombie shows that sprang up in the wake of the show’s critical and commercial success. AMC’s adaptation of Robert Kirkman’s seminal comic book series revived the entire zombie genre on the small screen, although it was still tough for other creators to offer original spins on the premise.

While a version of The Walking Dead that didn’t feature zombies sounded like a tough sell, more than one critically acclaimed show managed to pull off something akin to this premise. HBO’s incredible adaptation Station Eleven was a post-apocalyptic drama that was no less engaging and enthralling for its lack of undead antagonists, while another TNT show currently streaming on Netflix replaced zombies with aliens. Produced by Steven Spielberg, Falling Skies lasted 52 episodes across five seasons.

Set six months after aliens invade Earth and wipe out humanity’s power grid before killing nine in every ten humans, Falling Skies has a bleak, brutal premise. With only 10% of the world’s population left scattered across Earth, the characters of Falling Skies live in constant fear of attacks by both “Mechs,” the mechanical drones utilized by the aliens, and “Skitters,” their six-legged extraterrestrial pilots.

It is with this context that Noah Wyle’s Tom Mason, Moon Bloodgood’s Anne Glass-Mason, and a handful of other surviving humans try to eke out an existence and, eventually, hopefully, mount a resistance against the monsters. Arriving before Apple TV sci-fi shows like Sugar, Foundation, Invasion, Silo, and Severance started committing massive budgets to the genre, Falling Skies looks surprisingly good given its vintage.

Early seasons of Falling Skies wisely opt to keep the screen time of the aliens to a minimum, aiming for a siege story that feels akin to Spielberg’s 2005 adaptation of War of the Worlds or even director Frank Darabont’s seminal Stephen King movie The Mist. The relationships between human survivors are as important as the rare glimpses viewers do get of the alien threat, and Falling Skies manages to make its familiar dystopian plot feel compelling while still drip-feeding clues about the extraterrestrials and their intentions.

Since Falling Skies took a few seasons to even explain the motivations of its villains, the show could have lasted a long time without providing a definitive end to its story. However, the steadily worsening critical reception and increasing fan frustration surrounding later seasons of shows like The Walking Dead and HBO’s ambitious epic Game of Thrones proves that sometimes, narratives on this scale should quit while they’re ahead.

As such, it was arguably wise for the creators of Falling Skies to wrap up the show’s story with its strong fifth and final season in 2015. The last outing of Falling Skies not only offered satisfying explanations for any lingering mysteries surrounding the show’s aliens, but also gave a compelling conclusion to the arcs of the sci-fi show’s main characters and ensured its story felt cohesive and complete as a whole.

The Altruists: Julia Garner's Sensational Tragic Romance Gets A True Crime Disguise | Review

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Altruists presents itself as a true crime drama, which evolves into a tragic romance as the core characters continue to pull each other down.

The Altruists is a compelling eight-episode deep dive into the true story of the rise and fall of Sam Bankman-Fried and his viral crypto-trading platform. The series employs a non-linear approach to storytelling, peeling back layers of the narrative from the explosive and chaotic beginning, where the story ends, and working back through the circumstances and choices that led to SBF’s eventual downfall.

While unpacking the drama that surrounded SBF, FTX, and the sister company also largely owned by SBF, Alameda Research, The Altruists puts SBF’s relationship with Caroline Ellison front and center. Brought together through their love of math and dreams of becoming a part of the Effective Altruists (EA) community.

While The Altruists follows a true story, the perspective the show offers of two people’s tragic love story and the various glaring missteps that they took along that path is a fascinating one. With stellar performances from the leads and the wider cast, and constant tension throughout, The Altruists resembles a car crash because of the fact that it becomes impossible to look away before disaster strikes.

A common tool used when writing characters in novels and the written word is the unreliable narrator. A central figure, potentially even the protagonist, has a cloudy and misguided worldview that informs their reality and results in the reader being misled through their perspective.

While this method is often seen in books, it’s much more challenging to do the same thing on screen, when multiple characters all have a perspective to examine, and the narrative has to play out visually. That said, with both SBF and Caroline as the story's central characters, The Altruists makes incredible use of the less-than-trustworthy characters at its core.

The Altruists resembles a car crash because of the fact that it becomes impossible to look away before disaster strikes.

While other characters learn aspects of the truth, and it becomes clear relatively early on that there are details being withheld from the audience, it’s incredible how the tension remains. Small details are revealed in their turn without opening the floodgates and allowing the truth to tumble out all at once.

As much as the misdirection allows audiences to get caught up in the drama, it’s even more impressive to witness how the lack of honesty and clear communication impacts the characters in the show, especially SBF and Caroline.

As mentioned above, the story takes place across several moments in time, with the most prominent sections coming in 2018 and then in 2022. 2018 sees Sam and Caroline connect, spark up a relationship, and quickly struggle to establish their wants and needs while they cover up their emotions with the goal of earning exorbitant amounts of wealth in the hopes of donating it all to EA charities.

And examining their motivations and the driving force that pushes them to build extraordinary businesses, it would be easy to believe them to be good at heart. However, The Altruists also does a remarkable job of exposing how they justified actions that would cause most people’s skin to crawl, as they pushed each other further towards their financial goals while ignoring more serious personal subjects.

This creates a larger tension in the series and sees the chaotic nature of Sam and Caroline’s relationship bleed into every aspect of the lives of the people around them, creating a perfect storm that gave rise to this true story. But there is one notable issue with the way The Altruists approaches its story.

While eight episodes that all clock in at roughly an hour each may seem like plenty of time, The Altruists has an incredibly tight narrative that advances at pace from episode to episode. Beyond this, each episode is prone to shift multiple times through the years, which can at times be confusing.

The result is a wonderful true crime thriller that captures the emotion of a tragic romance.

There are clear signs on screen for when each shift happens, including the overt placement of the year on screen, and the more subtle detail of warmer colors for the past and cooler, more neutral colors for the present. Still, the constant changes in setting can be a little distracting at times, though it does create interesting connections between the past and present of the narrative.

Regardless, the tight pacing, the tension that looms over every episode, and the gradual reveals that allow the viewer to experience revelations and new obstacles in real time create a dynamic story that is thrilling to watch. This is further enhanced by the extraordinary performances of Julia Garner as Caroline Ellison and Anthony Boyle as SBF, alongside a strong supporting cast who all get moments to shine and stand out. The result is a wonderful true crime thriller that captures the emotion of a tragic romance, and ultimately, that makes The Altruists all the more compelling.

All eight episodes of The Altruists will be available to stream on Netflix from November 19, 2026.

The Altruists chronicles the rise and fall of Sam Bankman-Fried and Caroline Ellison, two young visionaries whose ambition to revolutionize the global financial system is overshadowed by allegations of an $8 billion fraud, casting them as modern-day Bonnie and Clyde figures in the eyes of Gen Z.

50 Cent Steps Out With Reebok G-Unit G6 Reboot

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The popular 2000s shoe line gets its global re-launch on Friday, Nov. 20.

Curtis “50 Cent” Jackson‘s reboot with Reebok is making a comeback, just in time for Christmas.

The hip-hop veteran recently teamed up with the sportswear specialist for the G-Unit G6, a blast from the past sneaker which were unveiled in August in its original red, white and blue colorway and proved to be a hit with 50 Cent’s followers. Those shoes re-launch globally on Friday, Nov. 20.

The basketball and street fashion-inspired gear initially rolled out in 2003, the year when Fifty blew up on airwaves and charts around the globe. The one that got away, “In Da Club,” logged nine weeks at No. 1 on the Hot 100, and the New Yorker followed it up two months later with “21 Questions,” featuring the late Nate Dogg, which spent a full month atop the national singles survey. His smash album Get Rich or Die Tryin’ held the top spot on the Billboard 200 for six weeks, and lent its name to the 2005 semi-autobiographical film, which gave 50 Cent his first starring role. Related Underscores Is First Transgender Woman Nominated for Best Dance/Electronic Recording Grammy Ella Langley Becomes First Act to Earn Top Two of TouchTunes Frontline Chart El Coyote Earns Third Regional Mexican Airplay No. 1 With 'Ranchero Enamorado' Explore 50 Cent See latest videos, charts and news

That partnership made Jackson one of the first musicians to lead a major footwear franchise. “Creating it the first time was special,” Jackson recounts in an interview with GQ. “I worked with Todd (Krinsky) and the team on everything and they really let me make it mine. It wasn’t just a sneaker—it became part of the culture.”

Krinsky, who started with Reebok in 1993, working in the mailroom, is now CEO. “Our relationship with 50 has always gone beyond sneakers,” he remarks in a statement. “He helped define an important chapter in Reebok’s history, and together we created something that had a lasting impact on sport style and culture. Bringing the G-Unit G6 back is an opportunity to celebrate that legacy while introducing it to a new generation.”

Tastes changed and by 2006 that original partnership had moved, with over four million pairs sold in those three years. Nostalgia and licensed kit, however, make for a powerful combination. “We’ve been talking about it for a while,” 50 continues. “Reebok is back, the G-Unit fans are still here, and there’s a whole new generation discovering us. The timing just felt right.”

The G-Unit G6 will be available from $130 at Reebok’s U.S. site, and for A$210 in Australia. “The takeover goes global,” reads 50 Cent’s social post. Check it out below.

HBO's 28-Episode Sci-Fi Masterpiece Pushes Mystery Box Storytelling To Its Absolute Limits

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Some of the greatest mystery box sci-fi shows are rare about giving concrete answers to viewers. Instead of providing instant resolutions to their mysteries, they master the art of keeping viewers guessing. And even when they do provide answers, they back them up with even bigger questions. Mastering this art of mystery-box storytelling, one HBO sci-fi masterpiece takes this approach to the extreme.

Based on Tom Perrotta's novel of the same name, The Leftovers is one of the strangest supernatural dramas out there. The show begins with a compelling hook where an event called the "Sudden Departure" causes the disappearance of 2% of the world's entire population. To put things into context, the total number adds up to 140 million, almost making it an apocalyptic event.

However, unlike other mainstream mystery box shows like Lost, The Leftovers remains less fixated on the grand mystery surrounding the disappearance and what caused it. Instead, it gradually evolves into a hard-hitting drama about the human condition and the resilience of the human spirit in the face of uncertainty.

The Leftovers could have been just another regular addition to the mystery thriller genre by following the framework popularized by shows like Lost. However, it chooses to defy the norm by weaponizing ambiguity and prompting viewers to question what one can do when facing an unsolvable mystery. The more it progresses, the more it becomes evident that there is no overarching conspiracy or alien invasion.

The "sudden departure" in The Leftovers cannot be reasoned with. As a result, the show's true mystery shifts to how its characters will handle their grief towards something they cannot logically explain. Since the outside world lacks answers, the survivors in the show turn inwards, facing their own desperate desire for meaning when nothing makes sense.

Most mystery box thrillers prompt viewers to come up with intriguing theories about what could happen next. The HBO sci-fi show highlights how every theory its characters come up with to explain their circumstances is only a coping mechanism. Religions and cults emerge in the show's story as characters put their own dogma and flawed justifications on full display.

Many viewers might initially find it hard to get past The Leftovers' early chapters. The show refuses to provide any concrete answers and feels a little too bleak with its portrayal of characters struggling to carry the weight of their guilt and grief. It is also not packed with one high-concept twist after another, which could be another massive turn off for hard sci-fi fans.

Still, The Leftovers deserves to be touted as a masterpiece because it intentionally chooses to be rough around the edges. It is a rare show that uses its mystery box storytelling as a solid narrative device to make viewers feel the same frustration and pain as its struggling characters.

Owing to this lack of tidy character arcs in The Leftovers, its brief moments of tenderness and humor also feel incredibly profound and memorable.

All in all, HBO's The Leftovers is far from being a conventional mystery box drama. It pushes the narrative device to its absolute limits, but not because it gradually runs out of stories to tell. The sci-fi series only does this because it intends to tell a meaningful story about the unexpected answers one finds when one learns to accept unanswered questions.

The Leftovers is set three years after 2% of the world's population mysteriously vanishes without explanation, following the residents of the small town of Mapleton as they struggle to rebuild their lives. As grief, faith, and unanswered questions reshape their community, those left behind search for meaning in a world forever changed by an event no one can explain.

Indigenous Queer Drama ‘Princess’ Is Dedicated to “Everyone Fighting for Their Freedom, Their Identity”

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Ecuadorian writer-director Christian Rojas' feature debut, which just premiered at Tallinn, is a contribution to the collective healing of wounds, both the creative's own and other people's.

Princess, or Ñusta in the Andean indigenous language Quechua, is not your standard coming-of-age film. At the heart of Ecuadorian writer-director Christian Rojas’ lyrical debut feature is Kallpa, 12, who has a big dream: to be chosen as Ñusta, the princess in the teenager’s Andean community’s Winter Solstice Festival. But having been born in the body of a boy, Kallpa is barred from taking part. Rigid tradition and community rejection mean that Kallpa must find a new home and family among a group of local trans women.

Kuri Fuerez stars alongside La Bruja de Texcoco, Bruno Giganti, Quispe Nuna, Romel Vargas, Kayita Quizhpe and Adele Pesantez. Princess, which is a co-production between Ecuador, Mexico, Argentina, Peru and Bolivia, features cinematography by Christian Hidalgo and editing by Jorge Macaya and Cristian Bermeo. The producers are María Ángeles Palacios and Daniela Fuentes Moncada. MMM Film Sales is handling sales. Related Stories Movies 'Sentimental Value' Casting Guru Recalls Meta Stellan Skarsgard and Elle Fanning Experience Movies Sarah Has 'One Month' to Leave Her Parents' Home. She's 45. (Exclusive Clip, Director Chat)

Princess, featuring dialogue in Spanish and Quechua, just celebrated its world premiere in the First Feature Competition of the 30th edition of the Tallinn Black Nights Film Festival (PÖFF) in Estonia.

After the premiere screening, Rojas and members of the production and creative team discussed Princess with the audience.

“Ñusta was born when I was 10 years old, after I had experienced sexual abuse that [affected] my childhood and my life for a long time,” Rojas shared. “I dedicate this film to those who have survived, to those who are still fighting, and to those who open their eyes every day [with] the strength to keep on [being] who they are – everyone fighting for their freedom, their identity, and their right to a life without fear.”Rojas lauded his collaborators as “the best team I could ever have,” concluding: “I say thank you to everyone for being part of it, part of healing this wound collectively – and not just my wound, but those wounds of everyone who’s still fighting and thinks they’re alone. We have to resist, raise our voice, and hug each other as a family.”

Safeguarding and ensuring a safe space, especially for the young actors, was a core focus for the Princess team. “The indigenous families … knew the story from beginning to end, but not the children,” Rojas said. “We had a psychologist on set and in pre-production, trying to develop this secure place for everyone so that we could actually enjoy making this movie.”

And the writer-director shared: “It was important to me for the children to feel like it was a game that they were playing, and that it was not a rigid process. So with this amazing team, what we did was play and go through it by playing.” One of the elements they used in the creative process with the kids was “a map of emotions,” Rojas said.

The Princess team also emphasized the importance of working in and with the local community with respect. “We didn’t want to extract anything,” the director emphasized. “What we wanted was real participation and reciprocity.”

The film team also included members of different indigenous communities, including some leaders within those communities who work on diversity and related issues.

The movie, set in 1997, includes an infamous real-life event that scarred Ecuador’s queer community that year. Police in the city of Cuenca back then raided a trans beauty pageant and arrested several people, who were then exposed to abuse and violence in custody.

“In terms of the visuals, we wanted to create the universe where Kallpa could travel, because this is a journey, and we wanted to always be with Kallpa on his journey,” Hidalgo explained. “And second, we wanted to be very close, so we are always with Kallpa.”

The sound also plays into Princess‘ aesthetic of closeness, allowing the audience to hear things we cannot see from a distance.

When it came time to screen Princess for the community, “I was really afraid,” Rojas shared. But he felt validated when “a 70-year-old lady stood up and said, ‘We need to talk about these stories here in our community.'”

6 Western Movies From the 2000s That Are 10/10 Masterpieces

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Over 100 years after the Wild West era officially ended, the genre continues to go strong. Maybe it's the mythic thrill of westward expansion or our fascination with the years immediately after the Civil War, but either way, the Western remains. Even in the 21st century, we continue to return to the 19th, hoping that by revisiting our nation's past we may be able to find some evidence to better understand our future. Or, maybe it's just as simple as that we love a classic shoot-'em-up — and there's nothing wrong with that.

When reviewing the Westerns of the early 2000s, a handful stood out as some of the most notable. From Oscar-winning adventures and popular remakes to revenge flicks and artistic dramas with little flare at all, the horse opera has always been able to command an audience. If you're looking for something to enjoy while riding across the dusty trail, then look no further than these stellar Westerns.

Okay, let's get this one out of the way right now. Not everyone will consider No Country for Old Men a Western. That much is true. The Coen Brothers adaptation of Cormac McCarthy's famed novel (and perhaps the best adaptation of his work) is, admittedly, a neo-Western, as it's set a century after the traditional "Wild West" period. Nevertheless, No Country is about as "modern Western" as you can get, with stunning West Texas aesthetics and a tension between lawmen, outlaw, and cowboy that speaks to the heart of the genre.

The brilliant cast includes Josh Brolin, Tommy Lee Jones, and Javier Bardem as all three center around the same payday in some form or another. There are moments in No Country that feel as if the audience is transported back to the late 1800s, and there are others that firmly set it in the 1980s. Either way, there's an argument to be made that this Best Picture winner is as vital to the modern Western canon of films as any old-school picture out there.

Although probably not a Western you thought you'd find on this list, Seraphim Falls is a stunning mix between a revenge-driven action flick and a survival picture set against an Old West backdrop. In the fallout from the American Civil War, Gideon (Pierce Brosnan) is on the run from former Col. Carver (Liam Neeson), who seeks his life after events that transpired during the war. With both men standing on opposite sides, they have no choice but to battle to the death.

Seraphim Falls is a highly-underrated Western. It's thrilling, bloody, and carried by the riveting performances of its leading men. With deeply spiritual undertones and strange themes one must decipher for themselves, Seraphim Falls reworks the classic "man vs. man" conflict into an engaging historical thriller, and it's better than most have ever given it credit for — and much of that can be credited to Brosnan and Neeson themselves.

Four icons and four completely different ways of walking into trouble. This is the one you would have been out there, why it fits, and what it would have cost you.

You have never explained yourself and you have never needed to, because people work out fairly quickly that you are the most capable person in the room without you ever having to mention it.

You are the funniest person in most rooms and the most loyal person in all of them, and you would be genuinely insulted if somebody suggested those two things were in any tension.

You never wanted this job, and you took it anyway because the alternative was standing there watching somebody less careful do it badly, which is the most Western reason there has ever been.

Nobody has ever successfully talked you out of something you had already decided was right, and a long line of grown adults has found that out the hard way.

While maybe not the most exciting of the films on this list, The Assassination of Jesse James by the Coward Robert Ford is perhaps the most stunning from a visual perspective. Andrew Dominik's visionary take on the Old West reinterprets the tales of outlaw Jesse James (Brad Pitt) through the eyes of Robert Ford (Casey Affleck), chronicling their relationship and the time leading to the former's death. Unlike the typical outlaw pictures, this is an introspective take on the genre that is more psychological than sensational.

Few Westerns offer the distinct visual style that Roger Deakins is able to convey here with his shot composition and masterful use of light and space. Of course, it's Affleck and Pitt who must then live up to those high standards by delivering complex and well-rounded performances that don't feel anything like Western caricatures. The runtime may be a bit bloated, but Jesse James is a radiant piece of cinematic work that offers something new for the genre.

A decade after he first wowed audiences with Dances with Wolves, Kevin Costner returned to headline the Wester with Open Range. When Boss Spearman (Robert Duvall) and his partner Charley Waite (Costner) find themselves on the wrong side of a range war with a local cattleman, they hold their ground in true genre fashion. But this isn't just a shoot-'em-up — Open Rance is also a romance, and Annette Bening steals the show.

Costner gave Duvall top billing for this picture, and anyone who sees it can understand why. The pair do some of their best and most underappreciated work in this Western, which gives them both real, meaty drama to work with — perhaps Duvall's best in the genre since Lonesome Dove. As a Western that packs a powerful punch, Open Range is a spectacular take on the Old West that captures everything that made the genre great in the first place.

When a longtime star like Ed Harris decides to take the Western genre on from behind the camera as well as in front of it, the result is a cinematic masterpiece like Appaloosa. When lawman Virgil Cole (Harris) and his partner Everett Hitch (Viggo Mortensen) arrive in the titular New Mexican town, they bring the fight to an unruly English cattleman (Jeremy Irons) who believes that the frontier is his to conquer.

Appaloosa is a love-letter to the traditional Westerns of the last century. With masterful character work from its leading men and simply gorgeous set design and costuming, it's one of those horse operas you'll want to watch again and again. Harris and Mortensen were born to play this sort of iconic Western duo, and there's a reason we're always a bit disappointed when the picture comes to an end.

Arguably the best Western remake ever put to screen, 3:10 to Yuma makes an already masterful Western even more so under the creative direction of James Mangold. When rancher Dan Evans (Christian Bale) is hired to take outlaw Ben Wade (Russell Crowe) to the titular train, they venture across the Old West on a journey neither likely knew the end of. The result is a high-octane, action-packed thrill that gives other genre entries a run for its money.

3:10 to Yuma is simply magnificent. It's the sort of blockbuster that will appeal to all audiences while still offering die-hard genre fans everything they came for. Bale and Crowe are a match made in cowboy heaven that breathes new life into an age-old Elmore Leonard story. If the original was a forgotten Western masterpiece that has aged perfectly, then the remake is all that and so much more.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Glen Powell Is a Has-Been Country Singer in Judd Apatow’s ‘Comeback King’ Trailer

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Cristin Milioti and Madelyn Cline also star in the Universal comedy that includes appearances from such performers as Reba McEntire, Jelly Roll and Shaboozey.

Glen Powell is a country star whose career hits a sour note in the trailer for filmmaker Judd Apatow‘s forthcoming comedy The Comeback King.

Apatow helmed the feature that Universal Pictures is set to release theatrically on Feb. 5. Cristin Milioti, Madelyn Cline, Stavros Halkias, Li Jin Hao and Kumail Nanjiani round out the cast.

The Comeback King centers on Powell as Aubrey King, a country star whose reputation changes after a false rumor that he cheated on his pop-star girlfriend (Cline). To restore his reputation, King sets out on a road to redemption that requires him to step out of the spotlight and finally earn his college degree. Related Stories Movies Christopher Briney and Michelle Randolph Star in Trailer for Prime Video's Holiday Rom-Com 'Clashing Through the Snow' Movies Christopher Nolan's 'The Odyssey' Will Return to Theaters Next Month as Awards Season Heats Up

“I’m looking for a comeback here,” Powell admits in the trailer. “I got real things on my heart. I got real things that I want to sing.”

Powell, who teamed up with Apatow to pen the script, trained in guitar and vocals to perform several original songs from top songwriters in the genre. The Comeback King features performances from such real-life country performers as Jelly Roll, Brad Paisley, Miranda Lambert, Shaboozey, Chris Stapleton, Brandi Carlile, Luke Bryan and Reba McEntire.

Apatow produces for Apatow Productions through its long-standing overall deal with the studio, while Powell and Dan Cohen produce for Barnstorm its first-look deal with Universal that was signed last year. Former Universal president of production Kevin Misher also produces for Misher Films. Barry Mendel and Josh Church executive produce.

Powell recently led the second season of Hulu’s Chad Powers and stars with Jenna Ortega in J.J. Abrams’ forthcoming movie The Great Beyond.

Apatow’s previous feature credits as director include Netflix’s 2022 film The Bubble, along with such titles for Universal as The King of Staten Island, Knocked Up and The 40-Year-Old Virgin.

Level Up Your Next Family Ski Trip at Banff Sunshine Village

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Sunshine Ski Village in Banff, Canada. (Photo: Sunshine Ski Village)

One of the best things about skiing with kids is that your definition of a great day changes. It stops being about vertical feet, powder depth, or how many black diamonds you can check off before lunch. Instead, it’s about finding a mountain where everyone can have fun together today while still having room to grow tomorrow.

That’s what makes Banff Sunshine Village such a gem for families. What really sets this ski destination  apart is the balance between beginner, intermediate, advanced, and expert terrain. All levels of skiers have plenty of runs to explore, making it easy for families of mixed abilities to spend the day on the mountain together. Across three connected mountains and more than 3,500 acres of terrain, there’s room to return season after season as confidence grows and skills evolve.

For many families, the sweet spot starts on the lower mountain around the Wolverine and Jackrabbit chairs. These areas feel like they were designed by people who know what it’s like to ski with enthusiastic kids. Runs like Miss Gratz, Rollercoaster, Kids Play, Wolverine, and Blue Bell offer the perfect mix of gentle pitches, playful rollers, natural features, and kid-approved tree skiing. Instead of simply sliding down groomers, young skiers and riders get to explore terrain that quietly teaches balance, turning, and confidence while they’re busy having fun.

That natural terrain approach creates a progression that feels effortless. One season you have your kids between your own skis, the next they’re asking to duck into the trees or tackle steeper lines.

As abilities advance, Sunshine keeps delivering. Families ready to explore more challenging terrain can tap into Banff Sunshine’s Ski & Snowboard School, where lessons help skiers and riders build confidence well beyond the beginner stage. For those interested in freeride skills, specialized instruction and the resort’s beacon training area provide a chance to learn mountain safety fundamentals in a controlled environment before taking on bigger adventures.

The secret weapon might be staying slopeside at the Sunshine Mountain Lodge. Instead of ending the day with a drive back to town, families can stretch the adventure into the evening. Tobogganing, movie nights, hot tub bingo, and nightly s’mores give kids something to look forward to after the lifts stop spinning, while parents get a chance to relax and enjoy being fully immersed in mountain life.

The best family ski destinations aren’t the ones you outgrow. They’re the ones that grow with you. At Banff Sunshine, that’s exactly what happens.

Banff Sunshine Village is the premier ski and snowboard resort in the Canadian Rockies. It’s famed for being home of Canada’s Best Snow – light, fluffy, smoke-dry powder and for having the longest non-glacier season in Canada. The resort is typically open from early November until late May. Guests of Sunshine can elevate their Canadian Rocky Mountain experience by staying at Sunshine Mountain Lodge, the Banff National Park’s only ski in, ski out hotel.

Ex-Trump Adviser Jason Miller Accused of Harassing Baby Mama in Deposition, Attorney Calls BS

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

One of Donald Trump's top campaign advisers is facing some nasty allegations from his baby mama ... who claims he turned a recent deposition into a personal attack -- but his attorney says it's all BS.

According to court documents obtained by TMZ, Jason Miller's former flame, A.J. Delgado, claims he repeatedly harassed and insulted her during a November 9 deposition in their ongoing child support battle ... eventually pushing her to cut the questioning short.

Delgado alleges Miller referred to her as a "complete whack job," a "deranged person," and a "sick individual" during the deposition ... while also calling her a stalker and a security threat to his family.

She says she tried to keep things professional, but Miller allegedly continued laughing, texting on his phone, and refusing to answer basic questions about his finances.

Delgado also takes aim at the magistrate overseeing the deposition ... claiming he failed to rein Miller in and refused her request to contact the judge assigned to the case.

She says things got so heated she ended the deposition with roughly half her questions unanswered ... claiming she couldn't endure any more of the alleged verbal abuse.

But Miller's attorney, Sandy T. Fox, says Delgado's claims have absolutely no merit. Fox tells us ... “Since 2017, Ms. Delgado has repeatedly made false allegations against Mr. Miller, his counsel, members of the judiciary and those who are not part of her narrative."

The lawyer went on ... "Her conduct during Mr. Miller’s deposition is just part and parcel of her repeated abusive litigation conduct, which resulted in members of the judiciary questioning her mental health and entering orders prohibiting abusive litigation conduct."

Fox added, "Her request for “additional pages” as a result of purported allegations that took place during a deposition that she voluntarily terminated was denied, as was her request to stay the trial court proceedings to delay the trial and continue the final hearing.

As TMZ previously reported, Miller and Delgado welcomed a son in 2017 after having an affair while working on Trump's 2016 presidential campaign. Miller -- who mysteriously resigned as communications director before the '17 inauguration -- was married at the time, and their relationship has since sparked years of bitter legal battles over their child.

And the courtroom drama is nothing new ... Miller reportedly took aim at a judge overseeing their paternity dispute in a social media post earlier this year.

6 James Bond Books That Robert Pattinson's Era Has To Adapt

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

James Bond is entering a new era, and that means the brilliant books that inspired the movie franchise will be adapted for the screen once again.

Robert Pattinson has officially been cast as James Bond, with Denis Villeneuve tapped to direct the upcoming Bond 26 movie, and there's no shortage of speculation about which Jame Bond story will be adapted first. In the wake of the beloved Daniel Craig era of James Bond, expectations are high for Pattinson and Villeneuve, with every creative choice under extreme scrutiny.

Watching modern spy thrillers before Bond 26 is a great way to prepare for the new era, and catching up on the novels and short stories that have been brought to life onscreen is just as important. Who the next James Bond will be and the kind of missions he embarks on are yet to be discovered, but Pattinson and Villeneuve have plenty of great writing to work with as they pave the way for the next Bond era.

One of Fleming's most enjoyable additions to the James Bond canon, Moonraker is a departure from some of the traditional tropes of the novels. Bond remains in England throughout the entire story and deals with his personal attachments to his country and his place in the world of espionage. The reflective nature of the novel differs from the movie version of the story, but both have their merits.

The 1979 film adaptation of Moonraker stars Roger Moore as Bond and is one of the most campy and outlandish additions to the franchise, and this could be an interesting angle for the next movies to take. In more recent years, James Bond has been a more serious, dramatically-oriented action series, but the origins of Bond are much more outlandish and fun, which could suit Pattinson's take on the character.

The reactions to Amazon MGM Studios' James Bond are in, as Robert Pattinson gives up his cape and cowl for a shaken and not stirred Martini.

Anxieties and tensions surrounding the development of weapons and the threat of the end of the world are higher than ever, and grappling with these fears through a thriller like James Bond makes them easier to contend with culturally. Full of high-stakes card games and the race to harness new weapons and technologies, Moonraker would make for a great next Bond film no matter the tone the film takes.

Moonraker is available for purchase here.

Not a book, but a short story: 007 in New York is one of the most under-the-radar works by Fleming, but there's something in the narrative that might appeal to modern audiences. Following Bond as he explores New York City and thinly developing some moments of action and intrigue, 007 in New York is almost a blank canvas for Bond 26 to explore.

Pattinson's Bond casting opens the door to a wide array of interpretations for the characterization of Bond and the kind of adventures he's going to go on in his next era. A more introspective, quirky Bond, like the one in 007 in New York, would fit right into Pattinson's persona as an actor. James Bond is known for its glamorous locations, and it would be a fun subversion of the franchise to see Bond in a grittier, more real version of New York.

Of course, Bond 26 could adapt a better-known short story, but taking on a story with the potential of 007 in New York would give Pattinson and the creative team the ability to make Bond whoever they want him to be. However, the next films will have to straddle the line between originality and the expectations of audiences, as every franchise must.

The collection featuring 007 in New York is available for purchase here.

Though Ian Fleming's Bond books started it all, the authors who have taken up the mantle of keeping the novels alive have done an excellent job. John Gardner's Scorpius is a novel full of betrayals, arms dealers, cults, and brainwashing, but this is just another day at the office for James Bond.

The titular Scorpius is a wildly fun villain, and there's no question that whoever is cast to play the antagonist in Bond 26 will have just as much riding on their performance as Pattinson. Scorpius features many pressing moral questions and revolves around the development of a cult engaging in a so-called "holy war." The thematic tension of Scorpius could easily be brought to life in 2026, though the novel was written in the late 1980s.

Harriet Horner, the Bond Girl featured in Scorpius, is another interesting character who could be modernized for the next movie. Her relationship with Bond and their fight against Scorpius is compelling, and there are many great actresses who could easily make Horner an even more complex role in the coming era of Bond.

Raymond Benosn's DoubleShot continues the fight between Bond and the Union, a terrorist organization featured in Benson's contributions to the Bond canon. Though it's always fun when Bond has an arch-nemesis to fight, there's a particularly sinister tension when Bond has to go toe-to-toe with a far-reaching criminal syndicate that he can't quite reach.

With Pattinson's history of playing offbeat characters and lending his talents to weird and interesting independent films, it will be important that Bond 26 pays tribute to Pattinson's ability to be a character actor, not just a leading man. DoubleShot makes use of a Bond double who the real 007 must foil, which would give Pattinson the opportunity to play both parts. It's no stretch of the imagination to envision Pattinson taking on a dual role in his first Bond project.

In the lead-up to the final confrontation with the double, the Union works to make Bond believe that he's hallucinating and losing his grip on reality. This is a scary feeling for Bond, and provides the opportunity for the kind of performance that Pattinson would shine in. There's room for experimentation in style and tone in any adaptation of DoubleShot.

DoubleShot is available for purchase here.

What better story to adapt for Pattinson's debut as Bond than a prequel that chronicles Bond's first mission with a license to kill, set before the events of Casino Royale. It would be a mistake for Bond 26 to produce another take on Casino Royale after Craig's era made such a definitive version in 2006. However, giving Pattinson a proper origin story and letting the audience get used to him could be a smart move for this Bond.

Though Forever and a Day takes place in the 1950s, it would be easy to translate the narrative to the present day, unless Amazon MGM wants to take a big risk and make the next Bond movies period pieces. Since that's unlikely, the more realistic chance the studio might take with Pattinson's Bond would be to develop him from his early stages as a spy instead of jumping into his adventures.

This would surely ruffle some feathers and might not be the direction the movie will go, but Forever and a Day has a strong story that sees Bond travel to the French Riviera to find out what happened to the 007 who came before him. Introducing beloved characters and demonstrating Bond's evolution, Forever and a Day could give viewers the chance to fall in love with a new Bond.

Forever and a Day is available for purchase here.

One of the most recent additions to the James Bond canon, the 2023 On His Majesty's Secret Service, was published to coincide with the coronation of King Charles III. Following Bond as he thwarts attempts on the King's life by a would-be usurper of the throne, On His Majesty's Secret Service isn't the most in-depth Bond book, but this creates the perfect opportunity for the film series to take the plot and expand it for a movie.

Topical and relevant, On His Majesty's Secret Service would open the door for Bond 26 to engage with the future of the monarchy in the modern era and show audiences what kind of Bond Pattinson is going to be. The 1963 book by Fleming, On Her Majesty's Secret Service, served as inspiration for the contemporary novel, and the film could draw from both stories in an adaptation.

The future of James Bond might be uncertain now, but with Pattinson in the driver's seat as 007, there's plenty of room for the movie series to bring timely works like On His Majesty's Secret Service to life.

On His Majesty's Secret Service is available for purchase here.

‘Grendel’ Animated Series Based On Dark Horse Comic Greenlighted At Netflix

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Matt Wagner‘s signature comic book character Hunter Rose is coming to the screen, this time for real. Netflix has ordered Grendel, an adult animated action series based on Wagner’s long-running comic book series published by Dark Horse.

Bobak Esfarjani (Widow’s Bay, WandaVision, Alien: Earth) is writer, executive producer and showrunner on the TV adaptation. In it, after the woman he loves is shockingly murdered, Hunter Rose devotes himself to one thing: vengeance. But as he tears through street gangs, the elite, and New York City’s supernatural underworld in pursuit of her killer, a chance at a real life forces him to choose: walk away from ultimate power, or become the very monster he hunts. Related Stories News 'Ginny & Georgia' Season 4 Premiere Date Set By Netflix With First-Look Photos Video Games 'Grand Theft Auto VI' Turns Chaos & Crime Into Cash & Clout With Record-Shattering Launch

Esfarjani executive produces with Chris Tongue, Tim Wiesch and Jay Komas for Dark Horse Entertainment (The Umbrella Academy), Lydia Antonini (Locke and Key, Halo: Forward Unto Dawn); and Wagner (Dead Boy Detectives). Bobbypills (Creature Commandos, Captain Laserhawk) is the studio. Watch on Deadline

“It’s incredibly exciting to see my creation finally make this leap to the animated screen! Netflix’s dynamic new series retains the source material’s dark adult themes and also brings a fresh and thrilling approach to the narrative,” Wagner said. “This is Grendel as I’ve always wanted to see the character portrayed — a hyper-kinetic level of action, bracing explosions of violence and a poignant engaging storyline.”

Netflix previously ordered a live-action Grendel series in 2021 as part of the streamer’s first-look deal with Dark Horse Entertainment, with Andrew Dabb as writer/executive producer, Abubakr Ali in the titular role and Jaime Ray Newman and Erik Palladino among the rest of the cast. A year later, Netflix opted not to move forward with the series.

The streamer remained high on the IP, and by early 2026, it had put in development an animated series adaptation, also under the first-look deal with Dark Horse, commissioning a writers room several months later.

“Grendel has been at the forefront of independent comics for over forty years, and we’re thrilled to bring Matt Wagner’s creation to Netflix,” said John Derderian, Netflix’s VP, Animation Series + Kids & Family TV. “Like the comic, this animated series will break the traditional superhero mold just as Matt’s time-honored comic book has always done. Bobak, Dark Horse Entertainment and Bobbypills are bold storytellers who love this world as much as its fans do, and we’re so excited to partner with them to bring this story to screen.”

Originally published in 1982 by Comico, Grendel was moved to Dark Horse following the indie label’s bankruptcy by Wagner who owned the character. Wagner is also known for creating the Mage comic book series and his work for DC Comics.

Esfarjani most recently served as a consulting producer on Season 1 of Apple TV’s Widow’s Bay, sharing in its Emmy win for Outstanding Comedy Series.

“Being able to bring a character as iconic as Grendel to life has been a true thrill,” he said. “Having the support of Matt Wagner, my great EPs, and the awesome team of Netflix, has made it all the more exciting, and partnering with Bobbypills Studio has made it all a true joy.”

Grendel joins Netflix’s slate of current adult animated series, which includes Blue Eye Samurai, Long Story Short, Haunted Hotel, Devil May Cry and Mating Season.

The Batman Part II Could Combine Victor Zsasz And The Court Of Owls

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Amid the rumors surrounding The Batman: Part II, the best solution might be to merge two truly disturbing Batman villains. Matt Reeves' The Batman established a particularly grim vision of DC's Gotham City, where corruption extends into almost every institution. Robert Pattinson's Bruce Wayne is still relatively early in his career as the Dark Knight, but his investigation into the Riddler's murders has already exposed just how deeply criminal activity is embedded in the city's foundations. The sequel has an opportunity to take that world in an even more disturbing direction.

With production underway and several new cast members joining Pattinson in The Batman: Part II, speculation about the story has intensified. Sebastian Stan, Charles Dance and Scarlett Johansson are among the additions, although their precise character assignments have not all been officially confirmed – despite what some outlets have reported. Meanwhile, leaked set photographs, production details and fan theories have prompted considerable discussion about the kind of antagonists Reeves might introduce.

One particularly interesting possibility would bring together two very different threats from Batman's comic-book history. Rather than giving the Dark Knight another serial killer to investigate or another powerful organization to expose, The Batman: Part II could connect the two, creating a conspiracy in which Gotham's most influential citizens employ an exceptionally dangerous individual to eliminate anyone threatening their interests.

One particularly interesting The Batman: Part II theory comes from Reddit user Low_Bridge_1141, who suggested that Victor Zsasz could effectively become the Court of Owls' personal assassin. The theory proposes that Zsasz might be employed to murder members of Mayor Bella Reál's task force, preventing them from uncovering the organization's criminal activities. Rather than operating independently, he would function as a hired killer carrying out the orders of Gotham's wealthiest and most powerful citizens.

It is an intriguing possibility because Zsasz is one of Batman's most disturbing adversaries. First appearing in Batman: Shadow of the Bat #1 in 1992, Victor Zsasz is a serial killer who carves a tally mark into his own skin for every person he murders. Unlike the Riddler, who wants Batman to recognize his intellectual superiority, Zsasz has no elaborate theatrical motivations – he simply enjoys killing.

The Reddit theory draws comparisons with Birthday Boy, a serial killer employed by the Penguin in the graphic novel Batman: Earth One. The suggestion is that Reeves could adapt a similar relationship, with a powerful Gotham criminal using a particularly vicious murderer to eliminate inconvenient witnesses.

The appeal of combining these antagonists in The Batman: Part II is that they represent two different kinds of corruption. Zsasz is an individual whose violence is intensely personal, while the Court of Owls is an institutional conspiracy that has allegedly influenced Gotham for generations. The organization consists of wealthy Gothamites who secretly manipulate the city through political influence, financial power, and murder. Its traditional assassins, known as Talons, are particularly dangerous opponents.

Giving Zsasz a similar position to the Talons would allow Reeves to preserve the grounded crime-thriller atmosphere of the first movie while introducing the much larger conspiracy hinted at. Rather than immediately presenting them, The Batman: Part II could establish the Court of Owls' influence through ordinary-looking people who use money and political connections to get away with murder.

It would also create an interesting problem for Batman. In the first film, Bruce investigates individual crimes and gradually discovers the connections between Gotham's criminal underworld and its supposedly respectable leadership. If Zsasz begins murdering members of Reál's task force, Batman could initially assume that the Penguin is responsible. Only after investigating the killings might he discover that somebody considerably more powerful is orchestrating events.

The Reddit discussion even proposes that Kenzie, the corrupt GCPD officer introduced in The Batman, could become Zsasz's first victim after the Court begins worrying that he might reveal information. That would give the sequel an immediate connection to the first movie while demonstrating how far the Court is prepared to go to protect itself.

Most importantly, the idea would give Pattinson's Batman an antagonist who is frightening for reasons beyond physical strength. The Riddler exposed Gotham's corruption, but Zsasz could demonstrate what happens when the people responsible for that corruption decide to silence anyone investigating them. Pairing an unpredictable murderer with a secret society that has spent generations manipulating Gotham would make the central conspiracy in The Batman: Part II particularly unsettling.

5 Robert Pattinson Movies That Prove He's 100% The Right Choice For James Bond

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Robert Pattinson is now confirmed to be the next James Bond, and these five fantastic roles show exactly why he's the perfect choice for the iconic role. After breaking through in the Twilight movies, Pattinson quickly built a fantastic resume of strong projects that showed off the actor's tremendous versatility, fantastic charisma, and great aptitude for choosing the right roles.

Most recently, Pattinson appeared in Primetime, where he stunningly captured the presence of the Dateline NBC host Chris Hansen. While the film had some detractors for how it framed certain real-life stories, Pattinson's performance at the core of the story has been almost universally praised.

Over the years, the James Bond movies have turned the 007 superspy into one of the most coveted roles in Hollywood. Following Daniel Craig's tremendous departure after No Time to Die, the franchise has set itself up for a new direction, with one of today's most celebrated directors, Denis Villeneuve, helming the new version of the series.

After much back and forth, and many considered names including Callum Turner and Josh O'Connor, the film has finally confirmed that Robert Pattinson, the biggest known star who auditioned for the role, will be Amazon MGM's next James Bond. While details of the film's story have not yet been shared, there is much to glean from Pattinson's body of work concerning his fit for the role.

An early hint at the kind of actor Pattinson would become, Cosmopolis is a tremendous showpiece for the star. Wrapped in the suave trappings of a James Bond film, Cosmopolis takes place almost entirely inside a stretch limousine, allowing Pattinson's performance to be given the central focus.

David Cronenberg's movie is clever and interesting, and examines the world of the ultra-wealthy with a sharp eye for critique. While Pattinson's Eric Packer has some distinct differences from a James Bond type, the character's outlook and style in this dense and thrilling drama hint heavily at Pattinson's career trajectory.

Released in 2012, the same year as the final Twilight film, this excellent movie is one of the first looks at Pattinson's dramatic chops and gives a great, early glance at the sleek, sophisticated presence that the actor would be able to bring to a role like James Bond.

The Odyssey is among 2026's best films, and it is filled to the brim with fantastic stars, including Matt Damon, Anne Hathaway, and Tom Holland. Despite such an enormous cast, it is Robert Pattinson who shines perhaps the brightest, delivering memorable lines as Antinous such as "get these beggars out of here".

Despite playing a role that is dastardly and villainous, as well as being only a supporting part of an enormous cast, Pattinson's incredible charm and charisma elevate everything about the character he plays. Certainly despicable, Antinous remains a bright highlight every moment he is on-screen, attesting to Pattinson's eminent watchability.

With The Odyssey​​​​​​, Pattinson proves that he is one of the strongest stars of his generation, and that he can shine, no matter the material. James Bond needs a charismatic and compelling presence for audiences to latch on to, and this film proves that he has the skill for that, no matter how the material frames the iconic spy.

Throughout his history in Hollywood, Pattinson has played his fair share of iconoclasts. Leaning into his unique edge, Pattinson has crafted unusual and uncomfortable characters in films like The Devil All the Time, where his natural good looks have made for a sharp and unsettling contrast.

With The Drama, however, Pattinson proves that he can also play more grounded and relatable characters.

Yes, James Bond is an unusual figure, and much of Pattinson's penchant for weirdness will be a good fit for the iconic spy. However, there must also be a sheen of regularity and accessibility to play such a charismatic role in such a long and storied franchise. His turn in The Drama shows he is perfectly capable of doing this.

Awkward and ordinary, Pattinson shows great chemistry with Zendaya in the film, bringing a leading man edge to the story's unusual and uncomfortable reveals. Pattinson still has the opportunity to go big with his performance in the film's end, but never stops feeling grounded and human in a way that many of his characters have not.

Robert Pattinson has provided a dark and curious examination of Bruce Wayne in Matt Reeves' The Batman, and the film does a great job of showing how well Pattinson can work as the core of a large franchise. In the movie, Pattinson steps into some enormous shoes of previous live-action Batman actors, including Christian Bale and Ben Affleck.

Despite the long shadow that the role has, Pattinson quickly rose to be among the best to ever wear the cowl.

The future of the DC Universe looks shaken recently, and with his casting as James Bond, Pattinson may not be available to play Batman many more times. Still, this role still shows how well a franchise and universe can be crafted around his central performance.

In addition, the grounded and dark realism of the movie, despite a blockbuster edge, seems to be in line with the kind of filmmaking that Denis Villeneuve relishes. Pattinson's turn as Bruce Wayne, then, sets up a great path for Pattinson to shine as James Bond.

Tenet may not be among Christopher Nolan's best movies, but the film worked especially well as a James Bond audition tape for Robert Pattinson. In Tenet, Pattinson shines as Neil, a British operative who works alongside the Protagonist, playing a role that allows him to flex his superlative charisma and charm.

The shadow of Twilight stuck with Pattinson for a long time, but Tenet helped to dispel that connection for many viewers, and prove that he has the potential to be much more than just Edward Cullen. While Pattinson did great work of this with projects like The Lighthouse, the wide reach of Tenet was instrumental in evolving his image amongst a wide audience.

In the 2020 time-bending thriller film, Pattinson is handsome, charming, sharply dressed, and incredibly suave. His tremendous wit is fully on display, making the role among the best in Pattinson's career to date.

Most important of all of this, Pattison remains a very likable and versatile star. While there is much to be revealed about the next James Bond story, films like Tenet have already shown that Pattinson can play a fantastic iteration of 007. As such, making the move to the James Bond franchise feels like a natural next step for him.
